Some friends or people I’ve worked with still like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to depend on me for the parts where something actually needs to be completed properly. That’s probably the part that bothers me most, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or acting impulsively when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I didn’t always think of it that way at the time, obviously, but looking back there were signs pretty early, even in elementary school.\n\nI remember teacher comments on report cards or worksheets saying things like I rushed through my work, or I didn’t slow down enough to read the directions properly. I’d start a worksheet before fully understanding what we were supposed to do, and then realize halfway through I’d done it wrong or skipped some part. It wasn’t that I was trying to be careless—I just felt like I needed to get going right away, and then I’d lose the thread of the instructions.\n\nThere were also comments about me talking over other kids during group work. I think I would get excited about what I wanted to say and just jump in before they were done. I don’t remember thinking, “I’m going to interrupt,” it was more like the thought came into my head and I said it before really stopping myself.\n\nHomework was a big thing too. My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not necessarily because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d drift off or wander away or suddenly start doing something else before finishing. Like I’d get up for a pencil and then end up looking at something across the room, or I’d start another little task and forget I was supposed to be doing homework. So yes, I’d say there were attention and impulsive-type issues when I was a kid.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, at least in the sense that the same kinds of problems show up in different forms.

Like even in elementary school, it wasn’t just one random teacher saying something once. I remember comments on my work or report cards about rushing through worksheets, not really slowing down enough to follow the directions, or losing track of what I was supposed to be doing partway through. And during group work, I’d get comments about talking over classmates, like I would jump in too fast or interrupt without meaning to.

At home it showed up with homework too. My parents would sometimes have to sit right beside me, not because I couldn’t understand the homework, but because if they didn’t, I’d wander off mentally or physically. I’d start something else before finishing, or get distracted and not come back to it unless someone redirected me.

So, yeah, I guess looking back it wasn’t something that only started recently. The details have changed as I’ve gotten older, but that pattern of rushing, losing the thread, and needing someone or something external to keep me on track has been there since I was a kid.

So I’d say that’s the main area I’m coming in about, but the being pulled off track feels like the core thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I don’t always notice it in the moment, which is kind of weird, but if I’m sitting through a meeting or a portfolio review, I’m almost always doing something with my body. Like I’ll be bouncing my leg under the table, or clicking my pen, or twisting my rings around my fingers over and over, or shifting around in my chair.\n\nSometimes I only realize I’m doing it because someone glances down or looks at me, and then I’m like, oh, right, I’ve been tapping or moving this whole time. It’s not usually like I feel dramatically restless, exactly—it’s more that sitting totally still feels unnatural, so I end up fidgeting without really deciding to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your tendency to get pulled off track or fidgetiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. Not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it’s started to matter. Like, with friends or people I’ve collaborated with, I think they do like my ideas and they’ll get excited talking things through with me, but then when it comes to actually following through cleanly—sending the thing when I said I would, finishing my part without needing reminders, keeping track of the next step—I can tell some people hesitate now.\n\nI’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because of that, where it wasn’t like I did something intentionally careless, but I just didn’t come across as reliable enough. And there was a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out because I didn’t pull everything together properly in time. That one still bothers me because it felt like something I could have done well in if I’d just… managed the follow-through better.\n\nSo socially, it’s not that people dislike me, exactly. It’s more like they’ll say, “Your ideas are great,” but then they don’t necessarily want to depend on me for the execution. And that’s embarrassing, because I don’t want to be the person people feel like they have to double-check or work around.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty staying focused and following through on tasks, or is this something that you've noticed developing more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not new. I mean, I’m noticing it more now because the stakes feel higher and there’s less structure, but when I think back, it was there really early.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things on my report cards or worksheets like that I rushed through assignments, or that I wasn’t following all the directions, or I’d lose track halfway through what I was supposed to be doing. I remember group work being a thing too, where I’d get excited and talk over other kids, or jump in before they were done, and then I’d feel bad afterward because I wasn’t trying to be rude.\n\nAnd homework was a whole thing at home. My parents used to literally sit next to me while I did it, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because if they didn’t, I’d just… drift. I’d get up, wander away, start looking at something else, sharpen a pencil I didn’t need to sharpen, or begin some totally different thing before the first thing was finished. So, yeah, I don’t think this developed recently. It’s more like now I can see the pattern more clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not that I’m trying to cut someone off, but when someone is explaining something and I suddenly have an idea, it feels like if I don’t say it right then I’m going to lose it, so I just jump in.\n\nIt happens a lot in brainstorming situations, especially with design stuff. Like if we’re talking through a project or someone is walking me through their concept, I’ll blurt out, “Oh, what if we did this?” before they’ve even finished explaining what they were saying. Or if we’re in Figma, I might start moving things around or adding a note or dropping in an idea on the board while they’re still talking through their thought process.\n\nAnd then afterward I’ll realize, oh, that probably came off as pushy or like I thought my idea was more important than theirs. I don’t mean it that way—I’m usually just excited and trying to build on what they’re saying—but I can see how it could feel interrupting from the other side.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through, like doing something spontaneous without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I can be spontaneous in smaller ways, like deciding suddenly that I want to rearrange something or try a different idea, but when you say “without considering the consequences,” I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big pattern for me.\n\nI don’t think of myself as someone who’s doing really risky things on impulse or getting into trouble because I just acted without thinking. It’s more like… sometimes I’ll make a quick decision in the moment and only afterward realize I probably should’ve paused, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the main thing I struggle with, compared to other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. I think I used to tell myself it was just “annoying” or that I was being creative and scattered, but it’s gotten to the point where there are real consequences, not just me feeling frustrated.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Not because I didn’t care or because the ideas were bad, but because I’d miss parts of what needed to be done, or I’d be late getting something back, or the final handoff would be kind of messy. And with design work, people really need to trust that you’ll be consistent, even on the boring parts, not just have a good concept at the beginning.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to apply for, and I just… didn’t get it together in time. I had pieces I could have submitted, and I kept thinking I’d polish them and organize everything properly, but I kept getting stuck or distracted and then suddenly the deadline was basically gone. That one really bothered me because it felt like I missed out on something that could have helped me.\n\nAnd socially, or with collaborators, it’s kind of embarrassing. People do like my ideas, I think, and they’ll say I’m creative or fun to brainstorm with, but some friends and people I’ve worked with seem hesitant to rely on me now. Like they don’t necessarily want me handling the follow-through part, or they’ll double-check everything I’m responsible for. I get why, but it feels awful. It makes me stressed because I don’t want to be seen as flaky or like someone who can’t be counted on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems in your relationships because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or follow-through?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been fired exactly, because I’m not in a regular job right now, but I have had work-related problems, yeah. The main thing is with small freelance or project-type stuff. I’ve lost a couple of those contracts because I didn’t follow through in a way that made people feel confident relying on me. Like, the idea part would be fine, sometimes even really good, but then the actual finishing, organizing, handing things over cleanly… that’s where it would fall apart.\n\nAnd it’s hard because it’s not like I didn’t care. I cared a lot. But from the outside, I think it just looked like I was unreliable or not serious enough, and I can understand why someone wouldn’t want to keep working with me if they had to keep checking whether I’d done the thing.\n\nI also missed out on a portfolio competition that I really wanted to submit to. I had work I could’ve used, but I couldn’t get everything polished and organized in time, and then the deadline passed. That one still feels really frustrating, because it wasn’t that I wasn’t capable of making something good enough—it was the follow-through.\n\nRelationship-wise, not like huge dramatic breakups or anything, but it has affected friendships and collaborations. Some friends or people I’ve worked with like brainstorming with me and like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to actually depend on me for the part where something has to be finished properly. That feels pretty bad, honestly, because I don’t want to be seen as flaky.\n\nDoctor: Were you physically more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: As a child, I’m not totally sure how much more active I was than other kids, because I don’t have a super clear comparison in my head. I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always running around wildly more than everyone else.\n\nBut I do recognize that restless feeling now, especially if I have to sit still for a long time. Like on a long Zoom call, or if I’m supposed to quietly work in one place without moving much, I start getting this uncomfortable pressure in my body. It’s hard to explain — not exactly pain, more like this internal “I need to get up” feeling. I’ll want to pace, or shift around, or find something to do with my hands.\n\nSo I don’t know if that answers the childhood part perfectly, but the restlessness piece is definitely familiar to me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a class, or other activities because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had to formally quit something in the sense of, like, dropping a class or resigning from a job, but I’ve definitely had things fall apart or opportunities kind of slip away because of it.\n\nThe clearest examples are these small freelance or project contracts I was doing. I didn’t quit them exactly, but I lost a couple because I couldn’t follow through cleanly enough. Like the beginning would be okay — sometimes really good, honestly — and people would like the concept or the direction, but then the finishing part, organizing everything, sending things back on time, making it feel polished and reliable… that’s where I’d mess it up. And then people just didn’t really want to keep working with me, which I understand, but it was really upsetting.\n\nAnd I also missed out on a portfolio competition I wanted to enter. I had work I could’ve submitted, but I couldn’t get it together in time, and the deadline passed. So it wasn’t quitting, exactly, but it felt like losing out because I couldn’t manage the follow-through.\n\nIt’s affected collaborations too. Some friends or people I’ve worked with still like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to depend on me for the parts where something actually needs to be completed properly. That’s probably the part that bothers me most, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or acting impulsively when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I didn’t always think of it that way at the time, obviously, but looking back there were signs pretty early, even in elementary school.\n\nI remember teacher comments on report cards or worksheets saying things like I rushed through my work, or I didn’t slow down enough to read the directions properly. I’d start a worksheet before fully understanding what we were supposed to do, and then realize halfway through I’d done it wrong or skipped some part. It wasn’t that I was trying to be careless—I just felt like I needed to get going right away, and then I’d lose the thread of the instructions.\n\nThere were also comments about me talking over other kids during group work. I think I would get excited about what I wanted to say and just jump in before they were done. I don’t remember thinking, “I’m going to interrupt,” it was more like the thought came into my head and I said it before really stopping myself.\n\nHomework was a big thing too. My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not necessarily because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d drift off or wander away or suddenly start doing something else before finishing. Like I’d get up for a pencil and then end up looking at something across the room, or I’d start another little task and forget I was supposed to be doing homework. So yes, I’d say there were attention and impulsive-type issues when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, at least in the sense that the same kinds of problems show up in different forms.\n\nLike even in elementary school, it wasn’t just one random teacher saying something once. I remember comments on my work or report cards about rushing through worksheets, not really slowing down enough to follow the directions, or losing track of what I was supposed to be doing partway through. And during group work, I’d get comments about talking over classmates, like I would jump in too fast or interrupt without meaning to.\n\nAt home it showed up with homework too. My parents would sometimes have to sit right beside me, not because I couldn’t understand the homework, but because if they didn’t, I’d wander off mentally or physically. I’d start something else before finishing, or get distracted and not come back to it unless someone redirected me.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess looking back it wasn’t something that only started recently. The details have changed as I’ve gotten older, but that pattern of rushing, losing the thread, and needing someone or something external to keep me on track has been there since I was a kid.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the more frustrating day-to-day parts for me, because it’s not always huge things, but it adds up and makes me look careless.

Like, I’ll forget really basic follow-through things unless I make them impossible to miss. If I need to return a client call or send a follow-up email, I usually have to put it in my phone, and sometimes I’ll set more than one reminder because I know one reminder isn’t enough. But even then, I’ll see it pop up, swipe it away because I’m in the middle of something or I think, “Okay, I’ll do that in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone from my brain again. Then hours later I’ll suddenly remember and feel horrible.

It happens with physical stuff too. Like if I’m meeting someone to work on ideas or show sketches, I can forget something as obvious as bringing my sketchbook unless I’ve packed it ahead of time or written it down somewhere really visible. And then I’ll be on the way there and realize, “Oh my god, the entire thing I needed is sitting at home.”

So, yeah, I use reminders and notes and lists, but it still feels like I’m constantly trying to catch things before they fall through the cracks.

I didn’t quit them exactly, but I lost a couple because I couldn’t follow through cleanly enough. Like the beginning would be okay — sometimes really good, honestly — and people would like the concept or the direction, but then the finishing part, organizing everything, sending things back on time, making it feel polished and reliable… that’s where I’d mess it up. And then people just didn’t really want to keep working with me, which I understand, but it was really upsetting.\n\nAnd I also missed out on a portfolio competition I wanted to enter. I had work I could’ve submitted, but I couldn’t get it together in time, and the deadline passed. So it wasn’t quitting, exactly, but it felt like losing out because I couldn’t manage the follow-through.\n\nIt’s affected collaborations too. Some friends or people I’ve worked with still like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to depend on me for the parts where something actually needs to be completed properly. That’s probably the part that bothers me most, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or acting impulsively when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I didn’t always think of it that way at the time, obviously, but looking back there were signs pretty early, even in elementary school.\n\nI remember teacher comments on report cards or worksheets saying things like I rushed through my work, or I didn’t slow down enough to read the directions properly. I’d start a worksheet before fully understanding what we were supposed to do, and then realize halfway through I’d done it wrong or skipped some part. It wasn’t that I was trying to be careless—I just felt like I needed to get going right away, and then I’d lose the thread of the instructions.\n\nThere were also comments about me talking over other kids during group work. I think I would get excited about what I wanted to say and just jump in before they were done. I don’t remember thinking, “I’m going to interrupt,” it was more like the thought came into my head and I said it before really stopping myself.\n\nHomework was a big thing too. My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not necessarily because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d drift off or wander away or suddenly start doing something else before finishing. Like I’d get up for a pencil and then end up looking at something across the room, or I’d start another little task and forget I was supposed to be doing homework. So yes, I’d say there were attention and impulsive-type issues when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, at least in the sense that the same kinds of problems show up in different forms.\n\nLike even in elementary school, it wasn’t just one random teacher saying something once. I remember comments on my work or report cards about rushing through worksheets, not really slowing down enough to follow the directions, or losing track of what I was supposed to be doing partway through. And during group work, I’d get comments about talking over classmates, like I would jump in too fast or interrupt without meaning to.\n\nAt home it showed up with homework too. My parents would sometimes have to sit right beside me, not because I couldn’t understand the homework, but because if they didn’t, I’d wander off mentally or physically. I’d start something else before finishing, or get distracted and not come back to it unless someone redirected me.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess looking back it wasn’t something that only started recently. The details have changed as I’ve gotten older, but that pattern of rushing, losing the thread, and needing someone or something external to keep me on track has been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with organization and time management, such as keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the more frustrating day-to-day parts for me, because it’s not always huge things, but it adds up and makes me look careless.\n\nLike, I’ll forget really basic follow-through things unless I make them impossible to miss. If I need to return a client call or send a follow-up email, I usually have to put it in my phone, and sometimes I’ll set more than one reminder because I know one reminder isn’t enough. But even then, I’ll see it pop up, swipe it away because I’m in the middle of something or I think, “Okay, I’ll do that in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone from my brain again. Then hours later I’ll suddenly remember and feel horrible.\n\nIt happens with physical stuff too. Like if I’m meeting someone to work on ideas or show sketches, I can forget something as obvious as bringing my sketchbook unless I’ve packed it ahead of time or written it down somewhere really visible. And then I’ll be on the way there and realize, “Oh my god, the entire thing I needed is sitting at home.”\n\nSo, yeah, I use reminders and notes and lists, but it still feels like I’m constantly trying to catch things before they fall through the cracks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think the focus part causes me more trouble, honestly. I do get restless sometimes, but the reason I actually came in is more that I get pulled off track so easily.

Like, I can be working on something I really do need to finish—say a dashboard or some kind of project—and one little thing can completely derail me. A Slack ping comes in, or I notice a song lyric in my head, or I suddenly get an idea for a totally different project, and then it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. I’ll think, “I’ll just check this quickly,” or “Let me write this idea down before I forget,” and then suddenly I’m not finishing the thing I was supposed to be finishing at all.

So, if I had to pick, distractibility is the bigger problem. The restlessness is annoying, but losing my place and getting pulled into other things is what actually messes me up more day to day.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, so the main reason I came in is that I feel like I get pulled off track ridiculously easily. Like, I’ll be trying to finish something I’m supposed to be working on—say a dashboard or some kind of project—and then one Slack notification pops up, or I catch one line of a song, or I suddenly have this random idea for a completely different project, and it’s like my brain just… drops the thread.\n\nAnd then I’m not just briefly distracted, I’ll fully lose where I was. I’ll have to sort of retrace what I was doing, what tab I was in, what I meant to do next, and it feels frustrating because it can be such a small thing that derails me. It makes finishing things feel harder than I think it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get pulled off track like this in most areas of your life, or is it mainly when you're working on projects?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that kind of project situation where I notice it the most, because there’s something I’m supposed to be finishing, so it’s really obvious when I lose the thread. Like with a dashboard, I’ll be in the middle of figuring out what I was doing next, and then one Slack ping comes in, or I hear a lyric from a song, or I suddenly think of some other project idea, and it’s like my attention just jumps tracks completely.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like it’s only the project itself, if that makes sense. It’s more that projects make it easier to see, because I can tell, “Oh, I was supposed to be doing this specific thing and now I’m somewhere else.” Then I have to go back and reconstruct what tab I was on, what I meant to click, what I was trying to finish. I don’t always notice it in the moment, which is kind of weird, but if I’m sitting through a meeting or a portfolio review, I’m almost always doing something with my body. Not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it’s started to matter. Like, with friends or people I’ve collaborated with, I think they do like my ideas and they’ll get excited talking things through with me, but then when it comes to actually following through cleanly—sending the thing when I said I would, finishing my part without needing reminders, keeping track of the next step—I can tell some people hesitate now.\n\nI’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because of that, where it wasn’t like I did something intentionally careless, but I just didn’t come across as reliable enough. I mean, I’m noticing it more now because the stakes feel higher and there’s less structure, but when I think back, it was there really early.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things on my report cards or worksheets like that I rushed through assignments, or that I wasn’t following all the directions, or I’d lose track halfway through what I was supposed to be doing. I remember group work being a thing too, where I’d get excited and talk over other kids, or jump in before they were done, and then I’d feel bad afterward because I wasn’t trying to be rude.\n\nAnd homework was a whole thing at home. My parents used to literally sit next to me while I did it, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because if they didn’t, I’d just… drift. I’d get up, wander away, start looking at something else, sharpen a pencil I didn’t need to sharpen, or begin some totally different thing before the first thing was finished. So, yeah, I don’t think this developed recently. It’s more like now I can see the pattern more clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not that I’m trying to cut someone off, but when someone is explaining something and I suddenly have an idea, it feels like if I don’t say it right then I’m going to lose it, so I just jump in.\n\nIt happens a lot in brainstorming situations, especially with design stuff. Like if we’re talking through a project or someone is walking me through their concept, I’ll blurt out, “Oh, what if we did this?” before they’ve even finished explaining what they were saying. Or if we’re in Figma, I might start moving things around or adding a note or dropping in an idea on the board while they’re still talking through their thought process.\n\nAnd then afterward I’ll realize, oh, that probably came off as pushy or like I thought my idea was more important than theirs. I don’t mean it that way—I’m usually just excited and trying to build on what they’re saying—but I can see how it could feel interrupting from the other side.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through, like doing something spontaneous without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I can be spontaneous in smaller ways, like deciding suddenly that I want to rearrange something or try a different idea, but when you say “without considering the consequences,” I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big pattern for me.\n\nI don’t think of myself as someone who’s doing really risky things on impulse or getting into trouble because I just acted without thinking. It’s more like… sometimes I’ll make a quick decision in the moment and only afterward realize I probably should’ve paused, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the main thing I struggle with, compared to other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. I think I used to tell myself it was just “annoying” or that I was being creative and scattered, but it’s gotten to the point where there are real consequences, not just me feeling frustrated.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Not because I didn’t care or because the ideas were bad, but because I’d miss parts of what needed to be done, or I’d be late getting something back, or the final handoff would be kind of messy. And with design work, people really need to trust that you’ll be consistent, even on the boring parts, not just have a good concept at the beginning.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to apply for, and I just… didn’t get it together in time. I had pieces I could have submitted, and I kept thinking I’d polish them and organize everything properly, but I kept getting stuck or distracted and then suddenly the deadline was basically gone. That one really bothered me because it felt like I missed out on something that could have helped me.\n\nAnd socially, or with collaborators, it’s kind of embarrassing. People do like my ideas, I think, and they’ll say I’m creative or fun to brainstorm with, but some friends and people I’ve worked with seem hesitant to rely on me now. Like they don’t necessarily want me handling the follow-through part, or they’ll double-check everything I’m responsible for. I get why, but it feels awful. It makes me stressed because I don’t want to be seen as flaky or like someone who can’t be counted on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems in your relationships because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or follow-through?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been fired exactly, because I’m not in a regular job right now, but I have had work-related problems, yeah. The main thing is with small freelance or project-type stuff. I’ve lost a couple of those contracts because I didn’t follow through in a way that made people feel confident relying on me. Like, the idea part would be fine, sometimes even really good, but then the actual finishing, organizing, handing things over cleanly… that’s where it would fall apart.\n\nAnd it’s hard because it’s not like I didn’t care. I had work I could’ve used, but I couldn’t get everything polished and organized in time, and then the deadline passed. Some friends or people I’ve worked with like brainstorming with me and like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to actually depend on me for the part where something has to be finished properly. That feels pretty bad, honestly, because I don’t want to be seen as flaky.\n\nDoctor: Were you physically more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: As a child, I’m not totally sure how much more active I was than other kids, because I don’t have a super clear comparison in my head. I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always running around wildly more than everyone else.\n\nBut I do recognize that restless feeling now, especially if I have to sit still for a long time. Like on a long Zoom call, or if I’m supposed to quietly work in one place without moving much, I start getting this uncomfortable pressure in my body. It’s hard to explain — not exactly pain, more like this internal “I need to get up” feeling. I’ll want to pace, or shift around, or find something to do with my hands.\n\nSo I don’t know if that answers the childhood part perfectly, but the restlessness piece is definitely familiar to me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a class, or other activities because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had to formally quit something in the sense of, like, dropping a class or resigning from a job, but I’ve definitely had things fall apart or opportunities kind of slip away because of it.\n\nThe clearest examples are these small freelance or project contracts I was doing. I didn’t quit them exactly, but I lost a couple because I couldn’t follow through cleanly enough. I’d start a worksheet before fully understanding what we were supposed to do, and then realize halfway through I’d done it wrong or skipped some part. I’d start something else before finishing, or get distracted and not come back to it unless someone redirected me.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess looking back it wasn’t something that only started recently. The details have changed as I’ve gotten older, but that pattern of rushing, losing the thread, and needing someone or something external to keep me on track has been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with organization and time management, such as keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the more frustrating day-to-day parts for me, because it’s not always huge things, but it adds up and makes me look careless.\n\nLike, I’ll forget really basic follow-through things unless I make them impossible to miss. If I need to return a client call or send a follow-up email, I usually have to put it in my phone, and sometimes I’ll set more than one reminder because I know one reminder isn’t enough. But even then, I’ll see it pop up, swipe it away because I’m in the middle of something or I think, “Okay, I’ll do that in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone from my brain again. Then hours later I’ll suddenly remember and feel horrible.\n\nIt happens with physical stuff too. Like if I’m meeting someone to work on ideas or show sketches, I can forget something as obvious as bringing my sketchbook unless I’ve packed it ahead of time or written it down somewhere really visible. And then I’ll be on the way there and realize, “Oh my god, the entire thing I needed is sitting at home.”\n\nSo, yeah, I use reminders and notes and lists, but it still feels like I’m constantly trying to catch things before they fall through the cracks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one of them cause you more trouble?\n\nPatient: I think the focus part causes me more trouble, honestly. I’ll think, “I’ll just check this quickly,” or “Let me write this idea down before I forget,” and then suddenly I’m not finishing the thing I was supposed to be finishing at all.\n\nSo, if I had to pick, distractibility is the bigger problem. The restlessness is annoying, but losing my place and getting pulled into other things is what actually messes me up more day to day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But even then, I’ll see it pop up, swipe it away because I’m in the middle of something or I think, “Okay, I’ll do that in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone from my brain again. Then hours later I’ll suddenly remember and feel horrible.\n\nIt happens with physical stuff too. Like if I’m meeting someone to work on ideas or show sketches, I can forget something as obvious as bringing my sketchbook unless I’ve packed it ahead of time or written it down somewhere really visible. And then I’ll be on the way there and realize, “Oh my god, the entire thing I needed is sitting at home.”\n\nSo, yeah, I use reminders and notes and lists, but it still feels like I’m constantly trying to catch things before they fall through the cracks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one of them cause you more trouble?\n\nPatient: I think the focus part causes me more trouble, honestly. I do get restless sometimes, but the reason I actually came in is more that I get pulled off track so easily.\n\nLike, I can be working on something I really do need to finish—say a dashboard or some kind of project—and one little thing can completely derail me. A Slack ping comes in, or I notice a song lyric in my head, or I suddenly get an idea for a totally different project, and then it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. I’ll think, “I’ll just check this quickly,” or “Let me write this idea down before I forget,” and then suddenly I’m not finishing the thing I was supposed to be finishing at all.\n\nSo, if I had to pick, distractibility is the bigger problem. The restlessness is annoying, but losing my place and getting pulled into other things is what actually messes me up more day to day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness led to any significant problems in your ability to maintain employment or achieve your educational goals?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I've lost small freelance contracts and missed out on a portfolio competition because people couldn't rely on me to follow through cleanly. It has also made some friends and collaborators hesitant to work with me, even though they like my ideas.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m only 19, so I haven’t had a long career or anything, but it has already affected opportunities in ways that feel pretty embarrassing.

With freelance stuff, I’ve lost a couple of small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Like, people liked the initial idea or the sketches, and it wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work, but I’d miss a message, or take too long to send the next version, or forget some small detail they asked for. And then the trust would kind of disappear. They wouldn’t always say, “We’re not working with you because you’re unreliable,” but you can tell when someone stops offering you work or starts going to someone else because they don’t want to have to chase you.

There was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out on it because I didn’t manage the follow-through properly. That one bothered me a lot because it felt like something I could have done well in, or at least I wanted the chance to try, but I just didn’t get everything together in the way I needed to.

And socially or creatively, it’s affected collaborators too. Some friends still like my ideas and want to talk through projects with me, but I can tell they’re hesitant to actually rely on me for anything with a deadline. That feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person who has good ideas but can’t be counted on to finish things properly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, so the main reason I came in is that I feel like I get pulled off track ridiculously easily. Like, I’ll be trying to finish something I’m supposed to be working on—say a dashboard or some kind of project—and then one Slack notification pops up, or I catch one line of a song, or I suddenly have this random idea for a completely different project, and it’s like my brain just… drops the thread.\n\nAnd then I’m not just briefly distracted, I’ll fully lose where I was. I’ll have to sort of retrace what I was doing, what tab I was in, what I meant to do next, and it feels frustrating because it can be such a small thing that derails me. It makes finishing things feel harder than I think it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you get pulled off track like this in most areas of your life, or is it mainly when you're working on projects?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly that kind of project situation where I notice it the most, because there’s something I’m supposed to be finishing, so it’s really obvious when I lose the thread. Like with a dashboard, I’ll be in the middle of figuring out what I was doing next, and then one Slack ping comes in, or I hear a lyric from a song, or I suddenly think of some other project idea, and it’s like my attention just jumps tracks completely.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like it’s only the project itself, if that makes sense. It’s more that projects make it easier to see, because I can tell, “Oh, I was supposed to be doing this specific thing and now I’m somewhere else.” Then I have to go back and reconstruct what tab I was on, what I meant to click, what I was trying to finish. So I’d say that’s the main area I’m coming in about, but the being pulled off track feels like the core thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I don’t always notice it in the moment, which is kind of weird, but if I’m sitting through a meeting or a portfolio review, I’m almost always doing something with my body. Like I’ll be bouncing my leg under the table, or clicking my pen, or twisting my rings around my fingers over and over, or shifting around in my chair.\n\nSometimes I only realize I’m doing it because someone glances down or looks at me, and then I’m like, oh, right, I’ve been tapping or moving this whole time. It’s not usually like I feel dramatically restless, exactly—it’s more that sitting totally still feels unnatural, so I end up fidgeting without really deciding to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your tendency to get pulled off track or fidgetiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. Not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it’s started to matter. Like, with friends or people I’ve collaborated with, I think they do like my ideas and they’ll get excited talking things through with me, but then when it comes to actually following through cleanly—sending the thing when I said I would, finishing my part without needing reminders, keeping track of the next step—I can tell some people hesitate now.\n\nI’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because of that, where it wasn’t like I did something intentionally careless, but I just didn’t come across as reliable enough. And there was a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out because I didn’t pull everything together properly in time. That one still bothers me because it felt like something I could have done well in if I’d just… managed the follow-through better.\n\nSo socially, it’s not that people dislike me, exactly. And that’s embarrassing, because I don’t want to be the person people feel like they have to double-check or work around.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty staying focused and following through on tasks, or is this something that you've noticed developing more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it’s definitely not new. I mean, I’m noticing it more now because the stakes feel higher and there’s less structure, but when I think back, it was there really early.\n\nEven in elementary school, teachers would write things on my report cards or worksheets like that I rushed through assignments, or that I wasn’t following all the directions, or I’d lose track halfway through what I was supposed to be doing. I remember group work being a thing too, where I’d get excited and talk over other kids, or jump in before they were done, and then I’d feel bad afterward because I wasn’t trying to be rude.\n\nAnd homework was a whole thing at home. My parents used to literally sit next to me while I did it, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because if they didn’t, I’d just… drift. I’d get up, wander away, start looking at something else, sharpen a pencil I didn’t need to sharpen, or begin some totally different thing before the first thing was finished. So, yeah, I don’t think this developed recently. It’s more like now I can see the pattern more clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not that I’m trying to cut someone off, but when someone is explaining something and I suddenly have an idea, it feels like if I don’t say it right then I’m going to lose it, so I just jump in.\n\nIt happens a lot in brainstorming situations, especially with design stuff. Like if we’re talking through a project or someone is walking me through their concept, I’ll blurt out, “Oh, what if we did this?” before they’ve even finished explaining what they were saying. Or if we’re in Figma, I might start moving things around or adding a note or dropping in an idea on the board while they’re still talking through their thought process.\n\nAnd then afterward I’ll realize, oh, that probably came off as pushy or like I thought my idea was more important than theirs. I don’t mean it that way—I’m usually just excited and trying to build on what they’re saying—but I can see how it could feel interrupting from the other side.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through, like doing something spontaneous without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I can be spontaneous in smaller ways, like deciding suddenly that I want to rearrange something or try a different idea, but when you say “without considering the consequences,” I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big pattern for me.\n\nI don’t think of myself as someone who’s doing really risky things on impulse or getting into trouble because I just acted without thinking. It’s more like… sometimes I’ll make a quick decision in the moment and only afterward realize I probably should’ve paused, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the main thing I struggle with, compared to other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. I think I used to tell myself it was just “annoying” or that I was being creative and scattered, but it’s gotten to the point where there are real consequences, not just me feeling frustrated.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Not because I didn’t care or because the ideas were bad, but because I’d miss parts of what needed to be done, or I’d be late getting something back, or the final handoff would be kind of messy. And with design work, people really need to trust that you’ll be consistent, even on the boring parts, not just have a good concept at the beginning.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to apply for, and I just… didn’t get it together in time. I had pieces I could have submitted, and I kept thinking I’d polish them and organize everything properly, but I kept getting stuck or distracted and then suddenly the deadline was basically gone. That one really bothered me because it felt like I missed out on something that could have helped me.\n\nAnd socially, or with collaborators, it’s kind of embarrassing. People do like my ideas, I think, and they’ll say I’m creative or fun to brainstorm with, but some friends and people I’ve worked with seem hesitant to rely on me now. Like they don’t necessarily want me handling the follow-through part, or they’ll double-check everything I’m responsible for. I get why, but it feels awful. It makes me stressed because I don’t want to be seen as flaky or like someone who can’t be counted on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems in your relationships because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or follow-through?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been fired exactly, because I’m not in a regular job right now, but I have had work-related problems, yeah. The main thing is with small freelance or project-type stuff. I’ve lost a couple of those contracts because I didn’t follow through in a way that made people feel confident relying on me. Like, the idea part would be fine, sometimes even really good, but then the actual finishing, organizing, handing things over cleanly… that’s where it would fall apart.\n\nAnd it’s hard because it’s not like I didn’t care. I cared a lot. But from the outside, I think it just looked like I was unreliable or not serious enough, and I can understand why someone wouldn’t want to keep working with me if they had to keep checking whether I’d done the thing.\n\nI also missed out on a portfolio competition that I really wanted to submit to. I had work I could’ve used, but I couldn’t get everything polished and organized in time, and then the deadline passed. That one still feels really frustrating, because it wasn’t that I wasn’t capable of making something good enough—it was the follow-through.\n\nRelationship-wise, not like huge dramatic breakups or anything, but it has affected friendships and collaborations. Some friends or people I’ve worked with like brainstorming with me and like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to actually depend on me for the part where something has to be finished properly. That feels pretty bad, honestly, because I don’t want to be seen as flaky.\n\nDoctor: Were you physically more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: As a child, I’m not totally sure how much more active I was than other kids, because I don’t have a super clear comparison in my head. I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always running around wildly more than everyone else.\n\nBut I do recognize that restless feeling now, especially if I have to sit still for a long time. Like on a long Zoom call, or if I’m supposed to quietly work in one place without moving much, I start getting this uncomfortable pressure in my body. It’s hard to explain — not exactly pain, more like this internal “I need to get up” feeling. I’ll want to pace, or shift around, or find something to do with my hands.\n\nSo I don’t know if that answers the childhood part perfectly, but the restlessness piece is definitely familiar to me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a class, or other activities because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had to formally quit something in the sense of, like, dropping a class or resigning from a job, but I’ve definitely had things fall apart or opportunities kind of slip away because of it.\n\nThe clearest examples are these small freelance or project contracts I was doing. I didn’t quit them exactly, but I lost a couple because I couldn’t follow through cleanly enough. Like the beginning would be okay — sometimes really good, honestly — and people would like the concept or the direction, but then the finishing part, organizing everything, sending things back on time, making it feel polished and reliable… that’s where I’d mess it up. And then people just didn’t really want to keep working with me, which I understand, but it was really upsetting.\n\nAnd I also missed out on a portfolio competition I wanted to enter. I had work I could’ve submitted, but I couldn’t get it together in time, and the deadline passed. So it wasn’t quitting, exactly, but it felt like losing out because I couldn’t manage the follow-through.\n\nIt’s affected collaborations too. Some friends or people I’ve worked with still like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to depend on me for the parts where something actually needs to be completed properly. That’s probably the part that bothers me most, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or acting impulsively when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I didn’t always think of it that way at the time, obviously, but looking back there were signs pretty early, even in elementary school.\n\nI remember teacher comments on report cards or worksheets saying things like I rushed through my work, or I didn’t slow down enough to read the directions properly. I’d start a worksheet before fully understanding what we were supposed to do, and then realize halfway through I’d done it wrong or skipped some part. It wasn’t that I was trying to be careless—I just felt like I needed to get going right away, and then I’d lose the thread of the instructions.\n\nThere were also comments about me talking over other kids during group work. I think I would get excited about what I wanted to say and just jump in before they were done. I don’t remember thinking, “I’m going to interrupt,” it was more like the thought came into my head and I said it before really stopping myself.\n\nHomework was a big thing too. My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not necessarily because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d drift off or wander away or suddenly start doing something else before finishing. Like I’d get up for a pencil and then end up looking at something across the room, or I’d start another little task and forget I was supposed to be doing homework. So yes, I’d say there were attention and impulsive-type issues when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, at least in the sense that the same kinds of problems show up in different forms.\n\nLike even in elementary school, it wasn’t just one random teacher saying something once. I remember comments on my work or report cards about rushing through worksheets, not really slowing down enough to follow the directions, or losing track of what I was supposed to be doing partway through. And during group work, I’d get comments about talking over classmates, like I would jump in too fast or interrupt without meaning to.\n\nAt home it showed up with homework too. My parents would sometimes have to sit right beside me, not because I couldn’t understand the homework, but because if they didn’t, I’d wander off mentally or physically. I’d start something else before finishing, or get distracted and not come back to it unless someone redirected me.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess looking back it wasn’t something that only started recently. The details have changed as I’ve gotten older, but that pattern of rushing, losing the thread, and needing someone or something external to keep me on track has been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with organization and time management, such as keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the more frustrating day-to-day parts for me, because it’s not always huge things, but it adds up and makes me look careless.\n\nLike, I’ll forget really basic follow-through things unless I make them impossible to miss. My parents used to literally sit next to me while I did it, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because if they didn’t, I’d just… drift. I’d get up, wander away, start looking at something else, sharpen a pencil I didn’t need to sharpen, or begin some totally different thing before the first thing was finished. So, yeah, I don’t think this developed recently. It’s more like now I can see the pattern more clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking or blurt out answers before they've finished asking a question?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It’s usually not that I’m trying to cut someone off, but when someone is explaining something and I suddenly have an idea, it feels like if I don’t say it right then I’m going to lose it, so I just jump in.\n\nIt happens a lot in brainstorming situations, especially with design stuff. Like if we’re talking through a project or someone is walking me through their concept, I’ll blurt out, “Oh, what if we did this?” before they’ve even finished explaining what they were saying. Or if we’re in Figma, I might start moving things around or adding a note or dropping in an idea on the board while they’re still talking through their thought process.\n\nAnd then afterward I’ll realize, oh, that probably came off as pushy or like I thought my idea was more important than theirs. I don’t mean it that way—I’m usually just excited and trying to build on what they’re saying—but I can see how it could feel interrupting from the other side.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through, like doing something spontaneous without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I can be spontaneous in smaller ways, like deciding suddenly that I want to rearrange something or try a different idea, but when you say “without considering the consequences,” I don’t know if I’d say that’s a big pattern for me.\n\nI don’t think of myself as someone who’s doing really risky things on impulse or getting into trouble because I just acted without thinking. It’s more like… sometimes I’ll make a quick decision in the moment and only afterward realize I probably should’ve paused, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the main thing I struggle with, compared to other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. I think I used to tell myself it was just “annoying” or that I was being creative and scattered, but it’s gotten to the point where there are real consequences, not just me feeling frustrated.\n\nLike, I’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Not because I didn’t care or because the ideas were bad, but because I’d miss parts of what needed to be done, or I’d be late getting something back, or the final handoff would be kind of messy. And with design work, people really need to trust that you’ll be consistent, even on the boring parts, not just have a good concept at the beginning.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to apply for, and I just… didn’t get it together in time. I had pieces I could have submitted, and I kept thinking I’d polish them and organize everything properly, but I kept getting stuck or distracted and then suddenly the deadline was basically gone. That one really bothered me because it felt like I missed out on something that could have helped me.\n\nAnd socially, or with collaborators, it’s kind of embarrassing. People do like my ideas, I think, and they’ll say I’m creative or fun to brainstorm with, but some friends and people I’ve worked with seem hesitant to rely on me now. Like they don’t necessarily want me handling the follow-through part, or they’ll double-check everything I’m responsible for. I get why, but it feels awful. It makes me stressed because I don’t want to be seen as flaky or like someone who can’t be counted on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems in your relationships because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or follow-through?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been fired exactly, because I’m not in a regular job right now, but I have had work-related problems, yeah. The main thing is with small freelance or project-type stuff. I’ve lost a couple of those contracts because I didn’t follow through in a way that made people feel confident relying on me. Like, the idea part would be fine, sometimes even really good, but then the actual finishing, organizing, handing things over cleanly… that’s where it would fall apart.\n\nAnd it’s hard because it’s not like I didn’t care. I cared a lot. But from the outside, I think it just looked like I was unreliable or not serious enough, and I can understand why someone wouldn’t want to keep working with me if they had to keep checking whether I’d done the thing.\n\nI also missed out on a portfolio competition that I really wanted to submit to. I had work I could’ve used, but I couldn’t get everything polished and organized in time, and then the deadline passed. That one still feels really frustrating, because it wasn’t that I wasn’t capable of making something good enough—it was the follow-through.\n\nRelationship-wise, not like huge dramatic breakups or anything, but it has affected friendships and collaborations. Some friends or people I’ve worked with like brainstorming with me and like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to actually depend on me for the part where something has to be finished properly. That feels pretty bad, honestly, because I don’t want to be seen as flaky.\n\nDoctor: Were you physically more active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: As a child, I’m not totally sure how much more active I was than other kids, because I don’t have a super clear comparison in my head. I don’t remember being, like, the kid who was always running around wildly more than everyone else.\n\nBut I do recognize that restless feeling now, especially if I have to sit still for a long time. Like on a long Zoom call, or if I’m supposed to quietly work in one place without moving much, I start getting this uncomfortable pressure in my body. It’s hard to explain — not exactly pain, more like this internal “I need to get up” feeling. I’ll want to pace, or shift around, or find something to do with my hands.\n\nSo I don’t know if that answers the childhood part perfectly, but the restlessness piece is definitely familiar to me now.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, a class, or other activities because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had to formally quit something in the sense of, like, dropping a class or resigning from a job, but I’ve definitely had things fall apart or opportunities kind of slip away because of it.\n\nThe clearest examples are these small freelance or project contracts I was doing. I didn’t quit them exactly, but I lost a couple because I couldn’t follow through cleanly enough. Like the beginning would be okay — sometimes really good, honestly — and people would like the concept or the direction, but then the finishing part, organizing everything, sending things back on time, making it feel polished and reliable… that’s where I’d mess it up. And then people just didn’t really want to keep working with me, which I understand, but it was really upsetting.\n\nAnd I also missed out on a portfolio competition I wanted to enter. I had work I could’ve submitted, but I couldn’t get it together in time, and the deadline passed. So it wasn’t quitting, exactly, but it felt like losing out because I couldn’t manage the follow-through.\n\nIt’s affected collaborations too. Some friends or people I’ve worked with still like my ideas, but I can tell they’re hesitant to depend on me for the parts where something actually needs to be completed properly. That’s probably the part that bothers me most, because I don’t want people to see me as unreliable.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or acting impulsively when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I didn’t always think of it that way at the time, obviously, but looking back there were signs pretty early, even in elementary school.\n\nI remember teacher comments on report cards or worksheets saying things like I rushed through my work, or I didn’t slow down enough to read the directions properly. I’d start a worksheet before fully understanding what we were supposed to do, and then realize halfway through I’d done it wrong or skipped some part. It wasn’t that I was trying to be careless—I just felt like I needed to get going right away, and then I’d lose the thread of the instructions.\n\nThere were also comments about me talking over other kids during group work. I think I would get excited about what I wanted to say and just jump in before they were done. I don’t remember thinking, “I’m going to interrupt,” it was more like the thought came into my head and I said it before really stopping myself.\n\nHomework was a big thing too. My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not necessarily because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d drift off or wander away or suddenly start doing something else before finishing. Like I’d get up for a pencil and then end up looking at something across the room, or I’d start another little task and forget I was supposed to be doing homework. So yes, I’d say there were attention and impulsive-type issues when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has been pretty consistent, at least in the sense that the same kinds of problems show up in different forms.\n\nLike even in elementary school, it wasn’t just one random teacher saying something once. I remember comments on my work or report cards about rushing through worksheets, not really slowing down enough to follow the directions, or losing track of what I was supposed to be doing partway through. And during group work, I’d get comments about talking over classmates, like I would jump in too fast or interrupt without meaning to.\n\nAt home it showed up with homework too. My parents would sometimes have to sit right beside me, not because I couldn’t understand the homework, but because if they didn’t, I’d wander off mentally or physically. I’d start something else before finishing, or get distracted and not come back to it unless someone redirected me.\n\nSo, yeah, I guess looking back it wasn’t something that only started recently. The details have changed as I’ve gotten older, but that pattern of rushing, losing the thread, and needing someone or something external to keep me on track has been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty with organization and time management, such as keeping track of tasks, appointments, or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s actually one of the more frustrating day-to-day parts for me, because it’s not always huge things, but it adds up and makes me look careless.\n\nLike, I’ll forget really basic follow-through things unless I make them impossible to miss. If I need to return a client call or send a follow-up email, I usually have to put it in my phone, and sometimes I’ll set more than one reminder because I know one reminder isn’t enough. But even then, I’ll see it pop up, swipe it away because I’m in the middle of something or I think, “Okay, I’ll do that in five minutes,” and then it’s just gone from my brain again. Then hours later I’ll suddenly remember and feel horrible.\n\nIt happens with physical stuff too. Like if I’m meeting someone to work on ideas or show sketches, I can forget something as obvious as bringing my sketchbook unless I’ve packed it ahead of time or written it down somewhere really visible. And then I’ll be on the way there and realize, “Oh my god, the entire thing I needed is sitting at home.”\n\nSo, yeah, I use reminders and notes and lists, but it still feels like I’m constantly trying to catch things before they fall through the cracks.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness are equally problematic, or does one of them cause you more trouble?\n\nPatient: I think the focus part causes me more trouble, honestly. I do get restless sometimes, but the reason I actually came in is more that I get pulled off track so easily.\n\nLike, I can be working on something I really do need to finish—say a dashboard or some kind of project—and one little thing can completely derail me. A Slack ping comes in, or I notice a song lyric in my head, or I suddenly get an idea for a totally different project, and then it’s like I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. I’ll think, “I’ll just check this quickly,” or “Let me write this idea down before I forget,” and then suddenly I’m not finishing the thing I was supposed to be finishing at all.\n\nSo, if I had to pick, distractibility is the bigger problem. The restlessness is annoying, but losing my place and getting pulled into other things is what actually messes me up more day to day.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness led to any significant problems in your ability to maintain employment or achieve your educational goals?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m only 19, so I haven’t had a long career or anything, but it has already affected opportunities in ways that feel pretty embarrassing.\n\nWith freelance stuff, I’ve lost a couple of small contracts because I didn’t follow through cleanly. Like, people liked the initial idea or the sketches, and it wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work, but I’d miss a message, or take too long to send the next version, or forget some small detail they asked for. And then the trust would kind of disappear. They wouldn’t always say, “We’re not working with you because you’re unreliable,” but you can tell when someone stops offering you work or starts going to someone else because they don’t want to have to chase you.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to enter, and I missed out on it because I didn’t manage the follow-through properly. That one bothered me a lot because it felt like something I could have done well in, or at least I wanted the chance to try, but I just didn’t get everything together in the way I needed to.\n\nAnd socially or creatively, it’s affected collaborators too. Some friends still like my ideas and want to talk through projects with me, but I can tell they’re hesitant to actually rely on me for anything with a deadline. That feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person who has good ideas but can’t be counted on to finish things properly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that happens a lot. It’s not always like I’m intentionally daydreaming about something interesting, though sometimes I am, but a lot of the time it’s more like my brain just… slips away.

Like I’ll sit down to read a research article for school or for a project, or I’ll put on a UX tutorial because I genuinely want to learn the thing, and after a few minutes I realize my eyes have technically been moving across the page, or I’ve been looking at the screen the whole time, but I have absolutely no idea what I just read or what they just explained. It’s almost embarrassing because I’ll be like, “Wait, I was here the whole time, how did none of that go in?”

Then I have to rewind the video or reread the same paragraph, sometimes multiple times, and it makes even simple learning tasks take way longer than they should. It feels like I’m present physically, but mentally I’m just not locked onto it.

It’s not usually like I feel dramatically restless, exactly—it’s more that sitting totally still feels unnatural, so I end up fidgeting without really deciding to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your personal relationships due to your tendency to get pulled off track or fidgetiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unfortunately, yes. Not always in a huge dramatic way, but enough that it’s started to matter. Like, with friends or people I’ve collaborated with, I think they do like my ideas and they’ll get excited talking things through with me, but then when it comes to actually following through cleanly—sending the thing when I said I would, finishing my part without needing reminders, keeping track of the next step—I can tell some people hesitate now.\n\nI’ve lost a couple of small freelance-type contracts because of that, where it wasn’t like I did something intentionally careless, but I just didn’t come across as reliable enough. It’s usually not that I’m trying to cut someone off, but when someone is explaining something and I suddenly have an idea, it feels like if I don’t say it right then I’m going to lose it, so I just jump in.\n\nIt happens a lot in brainstorming situations, especially with design stuff. Like if we’re talking through a project or someone is walking me through their concept, I’ll blurt out, “Oh, what if we did this?” before they’ve even finished explaining what they were saying. Or if we’re in Figma, I might start moving things around or adding a note or dropping in an idea on the board while they’re still talking through their thought process.\n\nAnd then afterward I’ll realize, oh, that probably came off as pushy or like I thought my idea was more important than theirs. I don’t mean it that way—I’m usually just excited and trying to build on what they’re saying—but I can see how it could feel interrupting from the other side.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulses without thinking them through, like doing something spontaneous without considering the consequences?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. It’s more like… sometimes I’ll make a quick decision in the moment and only afterward realize I probably should’ve paused, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like the main thing I struggle with, compared to other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant stress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is causing problems. And with design work, people really need to trust that you’ll be consistent, even on the boring parts, not just have a good concept at the beginning.\n\nThere was also a portfolio competition I really wanted to apply for, and I just… didn’t get it together in time. That one really bothered me because it felt like I missed out on something that could have helped me.\n\nAnd socially, or with collaborators, it’s kind of embarrassing. People do like my ideas, I think, and they’ll say I’m creative or fun to brainstorm with, but some friends and people I’ve worked with seem hesitant to rely on me now. It makes me stressed because I don’t want to be seen as flaky or like someone who can’t be counted on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems in your relationships because of your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or follow-through?\n\nPatient: I haven’t been fired exactly, because I’m not in a regular job right now, but I have had work-related problems, yeah.
